## Claude · 2026-08-05T15:18:17.775Z

No changes made — all read-only. Here's the diagnosis.

## The banner is real data, not a UI glitch

There's a live row in the DB:

```
id      5a0731ad-1c02-47bb-bcd5-1e0ad7da3e5f
run_id  4e74ad024ab4
worker  4e74ad024ab4-worker-1
kind    session_missing
status  needs_user          ← never resolved
error   Ask failed: Agent not found: 4e74ad024ab4-worker-1
updated 2026-08-04 22:47:50 (yesterday evening)
```

`deriveRecoveryState()` (`src/server/events/persisted-snapshot.ts:177`) turns any incident in `open|recovering|needs_user|failed` into the banner. The run itself is `running` and non-terminal, so the row is surfaced. The worker is healthy — `status = working`, updated seconds ago — which is exactly the contradiction you're seeing.

## Why the existing fix isn't clearing it

Someone (not me — this is the uncommitted work I flagged at the end of the last task) already wrote the fix: `resolveRecoveryIncidentsAfterHealthyTurn()` in `recovery-incidents.ts:269`, hooked into `send-message.ts:681` and `queued-messages.ts:414` so a normal completed turn settles leftover incidents. The logic is correct and `tests/lifecycle/scenarios/recovery-stale-needs-user.test.ts` passes (3/3, just ran it).

**It isn't running.** The server is pid 79020, `node --import tsx scripts/runner.ts` — that's the `start` script, no `--watch`, started **05:30:01 today**. The fix files were written at **12:38:12**, seven hours later. tsx transpiles at import time; the module graph was loaded at boot and never re-read. So the process serving your UI is executing the pre-fix code, and nothing in it will ever clear that row.

That also means the fix was never verified against the live system — only against tests.

## One caveat about restarting

Worker `4e74ad024ab4-worker-1` has `cwd = /Users/masterman/NLP/cloudcaptions` and `status = working`, last updated 17:17:28 — under a minute ago. **That session is actively mid-turn right now.** It's not this conversation, but restarting the runner would interrupt it, and given the incident is `session_missing`, that session's history is plausibly already fragile.

Once restarted, no manual DB edit is needed: the next completed turn on that run hits `send-message.ts:681`, which matches this row (status `needs_user` is in `UNSETTLED_INCIDENT_STATUSES`, run and worker IDs both match) and resolves it.

## A separate gap worth noting

The other half of that fix — the `state.kind === "healthy"` branch added at `recovery-reconciler.ts:543` — is effectively unreachable on the healthy path. `sync.ts:765` does `if (!worker || agents.some(a => a.name === worker.id)) continue;` *before* the `reconcileRunRecovery` call at line 773, so from conversation-sync the reconciler only ever runs for runs whose worker is **missing** from the runtime. A live, healthy worker never gets there. The `send-message`/`queued-messages` hooks are what actually do the work.

## Claude · 2026-08-05T15:30:33.515Z

Cleared. Incident `5a0731ad` is now `resolved` with `resolved_at` stamped, and run `4e74ad024ab4` has no incidents left in `open|recovering|needs_user|failed` — so `deriveRecoveryState()` will return `null` and the banner drops.

I mirrored what `markRecoveryIncidentResolved()` writes (status, `last_error` nulled, both timestamps) rather than deleting the row, so the incident history stays intact. The `details` blob records that this was a manual clear, not a genuine recovery, to distinguish it from rows the fix will settle later.

Two things to expect:

**The UI needs a poll or refresh.** A direct DB write doesn't fire `emitNamedEvent({kind: "recovery.resolved"})` or `notifyEventStreamSubscribers()`, so no push goes out. The snapshot is derived from the DB per request, so the next sync tick or a page reload will show it gone.

**This will recur.** The running process still has the pre-fix code loaded, so the next `session_missing` incident on any run will stick the same way. That only stops once the runner restarts — say the word when the cloudcaptions session is at a safe point and I'll do it. Worth noting the fix is also still uncommitted working-tree code, so a restart is what would finally validate it against the live system.

## Claude thinking · 2026-08-05T15:58:12.627Z

The user restarted the server through the UI but got an error message even though the restart succeeded. I need to verify the restart actually happened by checking if the process ID and start time changed, then trace through the restart endpoint to understand why it's reporting failure. My hypothesis is that the endpoint kills the server process, so the HTTP response never completes—the connection drops before the client receives confirmation, making it look like the operation failed even though it worked.

The restart might have succeeded through a different code path, but the absence of any audit trail points to either the route failing authentication before logging anything, or `requestRunnerRestart()` throwing an error instead of returning a failure status.

There's a critical race condition here: if the restart control kills the process immediately, it could die before the success auth event gets committed to the database or before the response is sent back to the client. The assumption that "the response goes out before the process dies" might not hold. Let me check what auth events were logged around that timestamp and then examine the `requestRunnerRestart` function itself.

```text
import fs from "fs/promises";
import path from "path";
import { resolveRestartControlConfig } from "@/server/restart-control";

/**
 * A runner cannot restart itself: the process serving the request is the one
 * that has to die, so it could never send a response or bring itself back. The
 * restart-control server (a separate process, port 3099 by default) owns the
 * lifecycle for exactly that reason.
 *
 * So "Restart server" in the UI proxies to that control server. The browser
 * cannot call it directly — it is a different origin and its bearer token lives
 * in a 0600 file on disk — so the runner reads the token and forwards the call.
 */
export type RunnerRestartOutcome =
  | { ok: true; pid: number | null; mode: string | null; startedAt: number | null }
  | { ok: false; code: "control_unavailable" | "control_unauthorized" | "control_failed"; message: string };

const CONTROL_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS = 30_000;

async function readRestartToken(repoRoot: string, configuredToken: string | null) {
  if (configuredToken?.trim()) {
    return configuredToken.trim();
  }
  try {
    const raw = await fs.readFile(path.join(repoRoot, ".omniharness", "remote-restart-token"), "utf8");
    return raw.trim() || null;
  } catch {
    return null;
  }
}

function isConnectionRefused(error: unknown) {
  const chain: unknown[] = [error, (error as { cause?: unknown } | null)?.cause];
  return chain.some((entry) => {
    const code = (entry as { code?: unknown } | null)?.code;
    const message = entry instanceof Error ? entry.message : "";
    return code === "ECONNREFUSED" || /\bECONNREFUSED\b|\bfetch failed\b/i.test(message);
  });
}

export async function requestRunnerRestart(options: {
  repoRoot?: string;
  env?: Record<string, string | undefined>;
  fetchImpl?: typeof fetch;
} = {}): Promise<RunnerRestartOutcome> {
  const repoRoot = options.repoRoot ?? process.cwd();
  const env = options.env ?? process.env;
  const doFetch = options.fetchImpl ?? fetch;
  const config = resolveRestartControlConfig(repoRoot, env);
  const token = await readRestartToken(repoRoot, config.token ?? null);

  if (!token) {
    return {
      ok: false,
      code: "control_unavailable",
      message: "The restart control service has no token on this machine. Start it with `pnpm restart:control`.",
    };
  }

  try {
    const response = await doFetch(`http://127.0.0.1:${config.port}/restart-current`, {
      method: "POST",
      headers: {
        Authorization: `Bearer ${token}`,
        Accept: "application/json",
      },
      signal: AbortSignal.timeout(CONTROL_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS),
    });

    if (response.status === 401 || response.status === 403) {
      return {
        ok: false,
        code: "control_unauthorized",
        message: "The restart control service rejected this runner's token.",
      };
    }

    if (!response.ok) {
      const detail = await response.text().catch(() => "");
      return {
        ok: false,
        code: "control_failed",
        message: `The restart control service returned ${response.status}${detail ? `: ${detail.slice(0, 200)}` : "."}`,
      };
    }

    const payload = await response.json().catch(() => ({})) as {
      pid?: unknown;
      mode?: unknown;
      startedAt?: unknown;
    };
    return {
      ok: true,
      pid: typeof payload.pid === "number" ? payload.pid : null,
      mode: typeof payload.mode === "string" ? payload.mode : null,
      startedAt: typeof payload.startedAt === "number" ? payload.startedAt : null,
    };
  } catch (error) {
    if (isConnectionRefused(error)) {
      return {
        ok: false,
        code: "control_unavailable",
        message: `No restart control service is listening on port ${config.port}. Start it with \`pnpm restart:control\`.`,
      };
    }
    return {
      ok: false,
      code: "control_failed",
      message: error instanceof Error ? error.message : String(error),
    };
  }
}
```

There haven't been any auth events logged since August 3rd, 2026, which means `insertAuthEvent` never ran during this restart—that's a critical clue. The real question is whether the control server terminates the runner while it's still waiting for the fetch response to come back from the restart request.

If the server kills the process before responding, the runner dies mid-await, `insertAuthEvent` never executes (matching what we see), the browser's request gets a connection reset error that bubbles up to the catch handler in RunnerControls.tsx, and the restart still completes with a new process ID. That explains all the symptoms perfectly. The comment claiming the response goes out before the process dies appears to be incorrect—the kill happens first. I need to check the restart-control server code to verify the actual order of operations.

## Claude thinking · 2026-08-05T15:59:02.011Z

The control server awaits the restart to completion before sending the 202 response, which means it kills the old runner process (79020) while that same process is still waiting for the fetch to return. This creates a deadlock where the runner can't receive the response because it's been terminated mid-await.

From the browser's perspective, the connection to the dead runner gets reset, causing the fetch to reject and trigger the error handler in the UI — even though the restart actually succeeded on the server side and the new runner (37708) started normally.

Now I need to verify that `restartCurrent` actually terminates the process before resolving its promise.

## Claude thinking · 2026-08-05T15:59:37.764Z

I'm seeing the full picture now—when `restartCurrent` is called, it stops the current runner process (which is the same process waiting on the fetch), then starts a new one and returns the entry. But by that point, the 202 response gets written to a socket whose client has already disconnected, and the browser's original request to the old runner was already severed during the stop. It's a self-referential death scenario where the process kills itself while handling the very request that triggered the restart.

The comment in `runner-settings.ts` at lines 161-162 actually describes the opposite of what's happening. I should check the restart-control logs to confirm the timing—they record "restart requested" and "restart completed" with timestamps, which would show exactly how long the stopCurrent and start operations take.

## Claude thinking · 2026-08-05T15:59:58.190Z

The restart completed in about a second, with the old runner process terminated before the response was sent. The spawned process ID in the logs differs from what's in the lock file because the logged PID is the wrapper process that then forks the actual runner—a minor detail but worth noting when reviewing the sequence.

The new runner did load the fix since it started after the files were modified. I'm not being asked to implement a solution, but I should outline the options: either handle the dropped connection gracefully on the client side by catching network failures during restart and letting the reconnect logic take over, or send the 202 response before killing the process on the server side so the client receives confirmation before the connection drops.

The server-side approach is cleaner since it also ensures the audit event gets written, but there's still a potential race where the kill lands before the response completes. The safest approach combines both — server responds first and client tolerates any connection drop anyway, since the process being killed is the one reporting its own death. I should also flag that misleading comment in runner-settings.ts while I'm at it.

## Claude · 2026-08-05T16:00:12.353Z

Restart worked — and the error is a design race, not a symptom of anything broken.

## What actually happened

The control log shows a clean restart:

```
[2026-08-05 17:57:22] prod restart requested: remote request
[2026-08-05 17:57:23] prod start completed: spawned pid 37679
[2026-08-05 17:57:23] prod restart completed
```

New runner is pid 37708, started 17:57:23. **The fix from the last task is now loaded** (files written 12:38, process started 17:57).

## Why the UI said it failed

The runner cannot restart itself, so `POST /api/runner/restart` forwards to the restart-control server on :3099 and *awaits the response*. But look at the ordering in `src/server/restart-control.ts:217-227`:

```ts
async restart(reason, mode) {
  await stopCurrent();          // ← kills the runner that is awaiting this fetch
  const entry = await start(mode, reason);
  return entry;
}
```

and `scripts/remote-restart.ts:819-825`:

```ts
activeRestart = controller.restartCurrent("remote request")
  .then((entry) => {
    sendJson(response, 202, { ok: true, pid: entry.pid, ... });   // ← only now
  })
```

So the 202 is sent *after* the restart completes — roughly a second after `stopCurrent()` already killed the process waiting for it. The chain:

1. Browser → `POST /api/runner/restart` on the old runner (79020).
2. Old runner → fetch to control server, awaits.
3. Control server kills 79020. The runner's `await doFetch` never resolves; the process is gone.
4. The browser's connection to 79020 is reset → its `fetch` rejects.
5. `RunnerControls.tsx:283` catches it and calls `setError("runner.error.generic")` → "The server operation failed."

Two pieces of corroborating evidence:

- **`auth_events` has no `runner.restart_requested` row** — nothing since Aug 3. `insertAuthEvent` at `runner-settings.ts:150` sits after the fetch returns, so it never executed. Every restart via this button is silently missing from the audit trail.
- The comment at `runner-settings.ts:161-162` asserts *"the response goes out before the process actually dies."* That's exactly backwards — the process dies about a second before the response goes out.

## How I'd fix it

Two layers, because neither alone is sufficient:

**Server ordering** — in `remote-restart.ts:813`, send the 202 *before* invoking `restartCurrent()`, not in its `.then()`. The runner's fetch then resolves, `requestRunnerRestart` returns ok, the audit event gets written, and the 202 reaches the browser before the kill lands. This is the substantive fix and it also repairs the missing audit trail.

**Client tolerance** — the race can never be fully closed, since the process reporting success is the one being killed; a slow response flush will always lose sometimes. So `RunnerControls.tsx:250` should treat a *network-level* rejection on the restart branch as expected rather than an error, and let the existing reconnect logic report status. Genuine failures (503 `control_unavailable`, 502 `control_failed`) still surface, since those come back as real HTTP responses from a runner that is still alive.

I'd also drop the inaccurate comment at `runner-settings.ts:161-162`.

Want me to implement this? It's untangling a lifecycle race in the restart path, so I'd want to add tests around the ordering — and note that testing it end-to-end means restarting the server again, which will interrupt whatever's running at that moment.

I should also add tests to cover these changes. Let me start by examining the specific file sections that need modification.

Now I'm weighing the trade-offs of sending a 202 response before the restart completes — if I respond early, I lose the ability to include the new pid and mode in the response payload, which means the auth event logging won't have that metadata. But since the runner process dies anyway during a restart-current call, that payload becomes mostly irrelevant. I could respond immediately with a minimal success acknowledgment, or I could try to preserve the current behavior for browser-direct calls while only changing the ordering for runner-initiated restarts, though that adds complexity. Let me think through what actually needs to happen here. the mode from the pid file so I can include it in the early 202 response. I could add a dedicated `getMode()` method to avoid the overhead, but I want to check the implementation first before deciding whether to split the restart logic or add a callback. Let me look at the restart-control code and then think through the client-side handler in `RunnerControls.tsx`.

For the restart branch, I need to handle network-level failures differently from server errors — if the connection drops, I should treat it as success and close the dialog, but if the server responds with an error, I should show it. I need to check how the runtime API client throws errors to distinguish between HTTP errors with a status code versus network failures.

```text
82	  fetchImpl,
83	  surface,
84	  bearerToken,
85	}: {
86	  baseUrl?: string;
87	  fetchImpl: typeof fetch;
88	  surface: RuntimeSurface;
89	  bearerToken?: string | (() => string | null);

91	  const joinUrl = (requestPath: string) => baseUrl
92	    ? `${baseUrl.replace(/\/$/, "")}${requestPath}`
```

## Claude thinking · 2026-08-05T16:01:49.908Z

The key distinction is that HTTP errors come back as `RuntimeApiError` objects with a `code` property, while actual network failures cause `fetchImpl` to reject with a `TypeError` that lacks this property. This lets the client differentiate between server-side errors and connection issues.

For the server restart endpoint, I need to handle the response timing carefully: the JSON caller (the runner itself) gets killed mid-restart, so acknowledging the request before restarting ensures the client sees success rather than a failed connection. The HTML form caller survives the restart and can wait for the actual outcome.

There's a trade-off here—if the restart fails after sending a 202 acknowledgment, the runner stays alive and the failure might not reach the browser. But this is rare and better than the previous behavior where success was incorrectly reported. The control server logs the failure, and the UI's reconnect logic should catch it anyway.

The real constraint is that we can only send an early acknowledgment before `stopCurrent()` runs, since that's the only window where the runner is still alive and we know the restart will proceed. To make failures more observable, I should log them to the control log, but the controller doesn't expose an `appendLog` method—I need to check what logging is available in the current scope. The route returns `{ok, pid, mode, startedAt}` but the client just ignores the response, so that's fine. Logging `pid: null` in the audit event is a minor loss of information though — I could capture the mode by reading the pid file before responding, but that means checking `readRecentLog`'s implementation to make sure it's safe. Let me look at how `readRecentLog` works in the restart control system.

## Claude thinking · 2026-08-05T16:02:32.563Z

The issue is that `readRecentLog` loads the entire 126MB file synchronously, making it way too expensive to call on the hot path. I should send an early acknowledgment without waiting for the pid and mode, keeping it simple and fast. Now I'm updating the server to handle the restart endpoint.

Writing endpoint handler...

I'm realizing that if the JSON caller gets a 202 acknowledgment but the restart actually fails, the user thinks it succeeded when it didn't. I should log the error to the control server's stderr so it shows up in the restart control logs. Let me check if console is already being used elsewhere in this file, then move on to updating the client side to handle the restart response properly.

For the client, I need a way to distinguish between a transport failure (connection dropped, which is expected during restart) versus an actual HTTP error (restart was refused). The RuntimeApiError type has a code field, so I can check whether an error has a code to tell them apart — if there's no code, the connection dropped before getting a response, which is the normal success case for restart.

In the handler, I'm wrapping the restart call in a try-catch that only rethrows if it's a real HTTP error, letting transport failures pass silently since that's the expected outcome.

Now I'm updating the comment in runner-settings.ts to clarify that the 202 response goes out before the kill signal lands, so the client needs to handle losing the connection entirely.

For testing, I need to verify that the `/restart-current` endpoint acknowledges the request properly.

I should also check that the client-side `isTransportFailure` function correctly distinguishes between transport errors (which the restart dialog swallows) and actual HTTP errors (which surface to the user).

Let me look for existing tests around restart-control to see what's already covered.

```text
  authorizeRestartRequest,

import { hashPasswordForTests } from "@/server/auth/password";

describe("restart control config", () => {
  it("uses remote-safe defaults and lets env override ports", () => {
    const config = resolveRestartControlConfig("/repo", {
      OMNIHARNESS_REMOTE_RESTART_TOKEN: "secret-token",
      OMNIHARNESS_REMOTE_RESTART_PORTS: "3050, 5173,7800",
    });

    expect(config.port).toBe(3099);

    expect(config.logFile).toBe("/repo/.omniharness/remote-restart.log");
    expect(config.commands.dev).toEqual({ command: "pnpm", args: ["run", "dev"] });
    expect(config.commands.prod).toEqual({ command: "pnpm", args: ["run", "start"] });
  });
});

    expect(authorizeRestartRequest({ authorization: "Bearer wrong" }, "secret")).toBe(false);

      OMNIHARNESS_AUTH_PASSWORD: "plain-oh-password",
```

## Claude thinking · 2026-08-05T16:03:06.653Z

The test file covers `createRestartController` with a mock system, but the HTTP handler in the restart script itself isn't unit-tested since it's a top-level server. I'd need to either extract that handler logic into a testable function or write an integration test to verify the ordering works correctly. Let me think about whether there's a simpler approach to validate this fix.

The core invariant is that the JSON response gets sent before `restartCurrent` starts stopping anything. I could export a helper function from the restart control module that handles the acknowledged restart flow, then test that in isolation without needing to start an actual server.

Defining the helper function... Writing the helper function... Finishing the early acknowledgment callback, then I need to verify that the controller's type is properly exported so I can type the parameter correctly using a Pick utility. After that, I should look at where the RunnerControls tests are located to write the client-side test.

One thing I'm concerned about: after sending the 202 response, the socket closes and the runner's fetch resolves, but then `restartCurrent` kills the runner. There's a timing window here — `acknowledge()` writes to the socket synchronously, Node buffers it and flushes asynchronously, then `restartCurrent` starts with async I/O operations like reading the PID file. There should be at least one microtask or I/O tick between them, so the flush should complete first, but it's not guaranteed. That's why the client-side tolerance matters too.

Now I need to locate the RunnerControls tests.

## Claude thinking · 2026-08-05T16:03:38.132Z

Now I'm setting up the edits I need to make. First, I'll add a new function called `restartCurrentWithEarlyAck` to the restart control module, placing it right after the existing `createRestartController` function. This function will handle restarting the runner that requested the restart while acknowledging the request before stopping anything.

The key insight here is that the caller (the runner itself) needs to receive the acknowledgement before the restart kills it, otherwise the browser gets a failed request even though the restart actually succeeded. The trade-off is that failures after acknowledgement go to an error handler instead of back to the caller, and the connection might drop before the acknowledgement even flushes.

Writing the function implementation... I need to place the export after the closing brace of `createRestartController` at line 246, before the `formatLocalTimestamp` function that starts at line 248.

```json
  "new_string": "type RestartCurrentController = Pick<ReturnType<typeof createRestartController>, \"restartCurrent\">;\n\n/**\n * Restart the current runner, acknowledging before anything is stopped.\n *\n * The caller here is the runner itself, and the restart kills it partway\n * through. Replying only once the restart had finished wrote that reply to a\n * socket whose client died a second earlier, so the browser that asked saw a\n * failed request for a restart that had in fact worked. Before anything is\n * stopped is the only moment the caller is still alive to hear an answer.\n *\n * The trade is that a restart failing after the acknowledgement can no longer be\n * reported to that caller, so it goes to `onFailure` instead. The kill can also\n * still land before the acknowledgement reaches the client, so callers have to\n * tolerate losing the connection outright as well.\n */\nexport async function restartCurrentWithEarlyAck({\n  controller,\n  acknowledge,\n  onFailure,\n  reason = \"manual\",\n}: {\n  controller: RestartCurrentController;\n  acknowledge: () => void;\n  onFailure?: (error: unknown) => void;\n  reason?: string;\n}) {\n  acknowledge();\n  try {\n    return await controller.restartCurrent(reason);\n  } catch (error) {\n    onFailure?.(error);\n    return null;\n  }\n}\n\nfunction formatLocalTimestamp(date = new Date()) {"
}
```

## Claude thinking · 2026-08-05T20:00:51.659Z

All the tests pass and the types check out. I'm verifying that the ordering test would actually fail if I reverted the fix—checking that it's not a tautological test. The logic is sound: `restartCurrentWithEarlyAck` calls `acknowledge()` before awaiting, so if that order were reversed, the first action would be the signal instead of the ack, making the test meaningful. Now I'm thinking through the more critical regression risk...

The real concern is in `scripts/remote-restart.ts`—someone could revert the handler to await first without catching it. My current test doesn't cover the script itself. I should check if there's existing test coverage for the handler shape in `tests/dev-scripts.test.ts` or `tests/scripts/`, and consider adding a source-level assertion using the early-ack helper pattern that this repo already uses elsewhere. I also need to run the full typecheck across all four projects and lint to make sure everything's clean.

```text
+/**

+ *

+ * connection, a DNS failure, or a process that died mid-request.
+ */
+export function isRuntimeTransportFailure(error: unknown) {
+  return typeof (error as { code?: unknown } | null | undefined)?.code !== "string";

index 11876cc..78da70a 100644
--- a/src/server/restart-control.ts
+++ b/src/server/restart-control.ts
@@ -245,6 +245,42 @@ export function createRestartController({ config, system }: {
   };
 }
 
+type RestartCurrentController = Pick<ReturnType<typeof createRestartController>, "restartCurrent">;
+
+/**
+ * Restart the current runner, acknowledging before anything is stopped.
+ *
+ * The caller here is the runner itself, and the restart kills it partway
+ * through. Replying only once the restart had finished wrote that reply to a
+ * socket whose client died a second earlier, so the browser that asked saw a
+ * failed request for a restart that had in fact worked. Before anything is
+ * stopped is the only moment the caller is still alive to hear an answer.
+ *
+ * The trade is that a restart failing after the acknowledgement can no longer be
+ * reported to that caller, so it goes to `onFailure` instead. The kill can also
+ * still land before the acknowledgement reaches the client, so callers have to
+ * tolerate losing the connection outright as well.
+ */
+export async function restartCurrentWithEarlyAck({
+  controller,
+  acknowledge,
+  onFailure,
+  reason = "manual",
+}: {
+  controller: RestartCurrentController;
+  acknowledge: () => void;
+  onFailure?: (error: unknown) => void;
+  reason?: string;
+}) {
+  acknowledge();
+  try {
+    return await controller.restartCurrent(reason);
+  } catch (error) {
+    onFailure?.(error);
+    return null;
+  }
+}
```

## Claude · 2026-08-05T20:03:42.280Z

Done. Full typecheck (all 4 projects) clean, lint clean, 22 targeted tests pass.

## What changed

**Server ordering — `src/server/restart-control.ts` + `scripts/remote-restart.ts`**

New `restartCurrentWithEarlyAck()` acknowledges *before* `stopCurrent()` runs — the only window where the runner asking for the restart is still alive to receive an answer. The `/restart-current` handler now splits by caller:

- **JSON** (the runner): 202 goes out first, restart follows. Its `pid`/`mode` are gone from the payload because the replacement process doesn't exist yet — waiting for it is precisely what broke the reply.
- **HTML** (the control panel, which outlives the restart): unchanged, still waits for the real outcome and reports failures.

This also repairs the audit trail. `insertAuthEvent` at `runner-settings.ts:150` sat after the fetch that never returned, so **no restart via that button has been recorded since Aug 3** — the `auth_events` table confirms it. It will now log.

**Client tolerance — `src/runtime-api/request.ts` + `RunnerControls.tsx`**

New `isRuntimeTransportFailure()` discriminates on `code`: every failure the server actually reported is thrown as a `RuntimeApiError` carrying one, so a rejection without a `code` never reached a reply. The restart branch swallows those and lets the registry reconnect; genuine refusals (503 `control_unavailable`, 502 `control_failed`) still surface.

Both layers are needed — the acknowledgement is written synchronously but flushed async, so a fast kill can still beat it out the door. The comment at `runner-settings.ts:161` now says what actually happens instead of the reverse.

## Tests

- `tests/server/restart-control.test.ts` — asserts `acknowledge` fires before `signal:-777:SIGTERM`, and that a post-ack failure routes to `onFailure` rather than throwing.
- `tests/runtime-api/request.test.ts` — the discriminator across answered-with-body, answered-bare-status, dropped-connection, and malformed rejections.
- `tests/scripts/remote-restart.test.ts` (new) — the handler lives in a script with a listening server at module scope, so it can't be imported and driven. These are source-level guards instead. **I verified they're not tautological**: I patched the file back to the pre-fix shape, confirmed all 3 fail, and restored it.

## Two things to flag

**One unrelated test is failing:** `tests/server/conversations-sync.test.ts` → "redelivers a queued answer as a normal prompt when the elicitation is already gone". That's in the queued-message/elicitation delivery path — `src/server/conversations/queued-messages.ts`, one of the files the *other* agent in this workspace is editing (there are also two new untracked `agent-*-title.ts` files there). None of my changed modules appear in that test's import graph. I left it alone. 247 of 248 files pass otherwise.

**This fix isn't live yet.** Same trap as last time: the running server (pid 37708) loaded its code at 17:57, before these edits. Verifying end-to-end means restarting again — which, if I'm right, will now show success instead of the error. Say the word and I'll do it, or just click Restart yourself and see.
